class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int

        rec, track = [0], defaultdict(int) 
        ct = start = ptr1 = ptr2 = 0

        if not set(s2).issubset(set(s1)): return 0

        s1 = ''.join(char for char in s1 if char in set(s2))
        
        while True:
            for char in s2:
                ptr = s1.find(char, start)
                if ptr == -1:
                    ct += 1
                    ptr = s1.find(char)
                start = ptr+1
            rec.append(ct + 1)

            if rec[-1] >= n1: return (len(rec)-2)//n2

            if ptr not in track: track[ptr] = len(0rec)-1
            
            else: break
        
        cycleStart = rec[track[ptr]]
        cycle1, cycle2 = ct+1 - cycleStart, len(rec)-1 - track[ptr]
        rest = n1 - cycleStart
        
        rem = cycleStart + rest%cycle1

        while rec[ptr2] <= rem: ptr2+= 1

        return (cycle2 * (rest//cycle1) + ptr2-2)//n2

def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1, n1, s2, n2):
    if not set(s2) <= set(s1): return 0
    repeatCount = [0]*(len(s2)+1)
    nextIndex = [0]*(len(s2)+1)
    j, count = 0, 0
    for k in range(1, n1+1):
        for i in range(len(s1)):
            if s1[i] == s2[j]:
                j += 1
                if j == len(s2):
                    j = 0
                    count += 1
        repeatCount[k] = count
        nextIndex[k] = j
        for start in range(k):
            if nextIndex[start] == j:
                prevCount = repeatCount[start]
                patternCount = (repeatCount[k] - repeatCount[start]) * ((n1 - start)//(k-start))
                remainCount = repeatCount[start + (n1-start)%(k-start)] - prevCount
                return (prevCount + patternCount + remainCount) // n2
    return repeatCount[n1] // n2

class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int

        rec, track = [0], defaultdict(int) 
        ct = start = ptr1 = ptr2 = 0

        if not set(s2).issubset(set(s1)): return 0

        s1 = ''.join(char for char in s1 if char in set(s2))
        
        while True:
            for char in s2:
                ptr = s1.find(char, start)
                if ptr == -1:
                    ct += 1
                    ptr = s1.find(char)
                start = ptr+1
            rec.append(ct + 1)

            if rec[-1] >= n1: return (len(rec)-2)//n2

            if ptr not in track: track[ptr] = len(0rec)-1
            
            else: break
        
        cycleStart = rec[track[ptr]]
        cycle1, cycle2 = ct+1 - cycleStart, len(rec)-1 - track[ptr]
        rest = n1 - cycleStart
        
        rem = cycleStart + rest%cycle1

        while rec[ptr2] <= rem: ptr2+= 1

        return (cycle2 * (rest//cycle1) + ptr2-2)//n2

```
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        if not set(s2).issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0
        repeatCount = [0] * (n1 + 1)
        nextIndex = [0] * (n1 + 1)
        j = 0
        count = 0
        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for i in range(len(s1)):
                if s1[i] == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1
            repeatCount[k] = count
            nextIndex[k] = j
            for start in range(k):
                if nextIndex[start] == j:
                    prevCount = repeatCount[start]
                    patternCount = (repeatCount[k] - repeatCount[start]) * ((n1 - start) // (k - start))
                    remainCount = repeatCount[start + (n1 - start) % (k - start)] - prevCount
                    return (prevCount + patternCount + remainCount) // n2
        return repeatCount[n1] // n2
```
This is known accepted. Need ensure indexing with `start + (n1 - start) % (k - start)` <= k? Let's verify. If n1 large and cycle found at k, start < k. Remainder r = (n1-start) % (k-start). start + r <= start + (k-start-1) = k-1, so within repeatCount. Good. If no cycle, return repeatCount[n1]//n2.

But is `repeatCount[start]` count after `start` copies of s1? Let's test. For k from 1 to n1, after scanning s1 copy k, count is number of s2 matched in first k copies. nextIndex[k] is index in s2 where next match starts after k copies. If nextIndex[start] == nextIndex[k] for start < k, then state repeats after k-start copies, and count increases by repeatCount[k] - repeatCount[start] per cycle. Then formula.

Let's write clean code:

```
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        if not set(s2).issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        repeat_count = [0] * (n1 + 1)
        next_index = [0] * (n1 + 1)

        j = 0
        count = 0

        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count[k] = count
            next_index[k] = j

            for start in range(k):
                if next_index[start] == j:
                    prev_count = repeat_count[start]
                    cycle_len = k - start
                    cycle_count = repeat_count[k] - prev_count
                    remaining = n1 - start

                    total_count = prev_count + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                    total_count += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - prev_count

                    return total_count // n2

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2
```

Need check formula with `remaining = n1 - start`. Suppose cycle found at k, start. We have `remaining` copies after start. Number of full cycles = remaining // cycle_len. Remainder copies = remaining % cycle_len. The count after start + remainder copies is `repeat_count[start + remainder]`. But wait `repeat_count` array is filled up to k, and `start + remainder` can be equal to k? If remainder = cycle_len? No, remainder < cycle_len, so start+remainder < k. Good. But if remainder=0, start+0=start. Then `repeat_count[start] - prev_count` = 0. Good.

But there is a subtlety: `repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - prev_count` counts matches in the first `remaining % cycle_len` copies after start, but if `remaining % cycle_len` copies include the cycle prefix, yes because state at start and at k are same. Since next_index[start] == next_index[k], the sequence of counts from start to start+r equals repeat_count[start+r] - repeat_count[start]. Good.

Need consider if `n1` is less than k? Loop only to n1. If no cycle found, return repeat_count[n1]//n2. Good.

k=1 scan "ab": count=1, j=0. nextIndex[1]=0, start=0 matches. remaining=5, cycle_len=1, cycle_count=1, total=0+1*5=5, return5. Good.

Correct. Note first copy produced no count but advanced j to 1; then each subsequent copy produces one count and ends at j=1. Good.

Let's define brute: S1 = s1*n1, count max m such that (s2*n2)*m = s2 repeated n2*m is subsequence of S1. Equivalent max c = number of s2 blocks in S1, return c//n2. The algorithm counts number of s2 blocks greedily. Greedy matching of s2 in S1 is optimal for subsequence? For counting repetitions of a fixed pattern, greedy earliest match of each character is optimal to maximize number of complete patterns. Yes.

Let's test with s1="abc", n1=3, s2="acb", n2=1. S1="abcabcabc". Can match "acb": a1,c3,b4? Wait b at2 before c, but after c at3, b at5? Actually positions: 1 a,2 b,3 c,4 a,5 b,6 c,7 a,8 b,9 c. Match a1, c3, b5 => one. Then a4? after b5, c6, b8 => second. Then a7,c9 no b => 2. Return2. Algorithm:

Let's craft final code with dict version:

```
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        if not set(s2).issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in set(s2))

        repeat_count = [0] * (n1 + 1)
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = 0
        count = 0

        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count[k] = count

            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle_len = k - start
                cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
                remaining = n1 - start

                total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]

                return total // n2

            seen[j] = k

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2
```

Need check `s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in set(s2))` computes set(s2) each iteration? Actually set(s2) inside generator is evaluated once? In Python, `set(s2)` is evaluated each iteration? The generator expression has `c in set(s2)`; `set(s2)` is evaluated each time? I think the expression `set(s2)` is evaluated for each element because it's part of the generator expression. Better assign `allowed = set(s2)` and use. Let's do:
```
allowed = set(s2)
if not allowed.issubset(set(s1)): return 0
s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in allowed)
```
Good.

```
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        allowed = set(s2)
        if not allowed.issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in allowed)

        repeat_count = [0] * (n1 + 1)
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = 0
        count = 0

        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count[k] = count

            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle_len = k - start
                cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
                remaining = n1 - start

                total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]

                return total // n2

            seen[j] = k

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2
```

If the same `j` occurred after `start` copies, then the process from copy `start+1` to copy `k` is a cycle that returns to the same `j` and increases `count` by `cycle_count`. Repeating this cycle `q` times gives `q * cycle_count` additional matches and consumes `q * cycle_len` copies. Then the remaining `r` copies after the cycles have the same behavior as from `start` to `start+r`, so add `repeat_count[start+r] - repeat_count[start]`. This is correct.

Let's think about if there is an even simpler accepted solution:
```
def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1, n1, s2, n2):
    repeatCount = [0]*(n1+1)
    nextIndex = [0]*(n1+1)
    j=0; count=0
    for k in range(1,n1+1):
        for i in range(len(s1)):
            if s1[i]==s2[j]:
                j+=1
                if j==len(s2):
                    j=0
                    count+=1
        repeatCount[k]=count
        nextIndex[k]=j
        for start in range(k):
            if nextIndex[start]==j:
                prevCount=repeatCount[start]
                patternCount=(repeatCount[k]-prevCount)*((n1-start)//(k-start))
                remainCount=repeatCount[start+(n1-start)%(k-start)]-prevCount
                return (prevCount+patternCount+remainCount)//n2
    return repeatCount[n1]//n2
```
This is widely known. Our dict version is equivalent and maybe less familiar but correct. Need ensure no hidden issue with using first occurrence vs scanning all previous. Let's prove more rigorously.

At end of each copy k, state `j_k` (next index in s2) and `c_k` (number of complete s2 matches). The transition from one copy to next is deterministic: `(j_k, c_k) -> (j_{k+1}, c_{k+1})`. Since `j` has finite states, if `j_a = j_b` for a<b, then because the transition depends only on `j`, the sequence of j states from a onward repeats with period d=b-a, and counts increase by `c_b - c_a` each period. Thus for n1 copies:
- Before cycle start a, c_a matches.
- Remaining copies = n1 - a.
- Full cycles q = remaining // d, remainder r = remaining % d.
- After q full cycles, count = c_a + q*(c_b-c_a).
- After r additional copies, since j_a = j_b, the count after a+r copies is c_{a+r} (if a+r <= b? Actually if r < d, a+r < b, and c_{a+r} is known because b<=n1? Wait b is current k <= n1. Since a+r < b <= n1, c_{a+r} is filled. The additional matches in the first r copies of the cycle are c_{a+r} - c_a. So total = c_a + q*(c_b-c_a) + (c_{a+r} - c_a). This matches formula. Good.

One subtlety: If `a+r` could be equal to `b` when r=0? no, r=0, a+r=a. If r>0, a+r < b. Good.

Now, if using `seen` dict first occurrence, `a` is first occurrence of `j_b`. It might be that `a+r` is less than `a`? no.

Let's test with a case where `j` repeats at start=0 and k=3, but `a+r` maybe equals k? If r = d-1, a+r = k-1, filled. Good.

Now, let's think about if `repeat_count` list length n1+1 and we access `repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len]` where `remaining = n1 - start`. Since `remaining % cycle_len` can be up to cycle_len-1, so index <= start+cycle_len-1 = k-1. Since k <= n1, index <= n1-1. Filled. Good.

Now, if cycle found at k=n1 and r = n1-start % cycle_len, index <= k-1, filled. Good.

Algorithm with dynamic list:
```
repeat_count = [0]  # count after 0 copies
seen = {0: 0}
j = 0
count = 0
k = 0
while k < n1:
    k += 1
    for ch in s1:
        ...
    repeat_count.append(count)
    if j in seen:
        start = seen[j]
        cycle_len = k - start
        cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
        remaining = n1 - start
        total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
        total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]
        return total // n2
    seen[j] = k
return repeat_count[n1] // n2
```
Need ensure `repeat_count` has index `n1` if no cycle and n1 maybe > len(s2)? But if no cycle, n1 <= len(s2) because j has len(s2) states and seen includes 0, so after len(s2) copies, if no repeat? Let's see possible distinct j values count len(s2). At k=0, j=0 in seen. At each k, if j not in seen, we add. There are len(s2) possible j values including 0. Since 0 already seen at k=0, if we never see a repeat, we can add at most len(s2)-1 new j values, so loop at most len(s2) copies. Thus n1 <= len(s2) if no cycle. So `repeat_count[n1]` exists because we appended each k up to n1. If n1 > len(s2), cycle found before or at len(s2)+1? Let's check: At k=1, if j=0, repeat found. If not, add j1. At k=2, if j=0 or j1, repeat. There are len(s2) possible j. With initial 0, after len(s2) copies, we have seen len(s2) distinct j? Actually if no repeat for len(s2) copies, we would have seen 0 plus len(s2) new j = len(s2)+1 distinct values, impossible. So repeat by k=len(s2). Wait if len(s2)=2, possible j {0,1}. Initial seen {0}. k=1 j=1 add. k=2 j must be 0 or1, repeat. So cycle found by k=2. Thus no cycle only if n1 < len(s2)? If n1=len(s2), repeat at k=len(s2)? Let's test len=3, possible {0,1,2}. Dynamic list code:
```
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        allowed = set(s2)
        if not allowed.issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in allowed)

        repeat_count = [0]
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = 0
        count = 0
        k = 0

        while k < n1:
            k += 1
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count.append(count)

            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle_len = k - start
                cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
                remaining = n1 - start

                total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]

                return total // n2

            seen[j] = k

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2
```
Need ensure `repeat_count[n1]` exists if loop exits without return. Since loop increments k until k==n1 and appends each time, length = n1+1. Good.

class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        allowed = set(s2)
        if not allowed.issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in allowed)

        repeat_count = [0]
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = 0
        count = 0
        k = 0

        while k < n1:
            k += 1
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count.append(count)

            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle_len = k - start
                cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
                remaining = n1 - start

                total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]

                return total // n2

            seen[j] = k

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2

```
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        if not set(s2) <= set(s1):
            return 0
        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in set(s2))
        count = [0]
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = c = 0
        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        c += 1
            count.append(c)
            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle = k - start
                add = c - count[start]
                rest = n1 - start
                ans = count[start] + add * (rest // cycle)
                ans += count[start + rest % cycle] - count[start]
                return ans // n2
            seen[j] = k
        return count[n1] // n2
```
This loops range(1,n1+1) and allocates list up to n1 if no cycle. But no cycle only if n1 <= len(s2), so list length small. Actually if n1 huge, cycle found early, but `count` list still allocated with `[0]` and appended only until cycle, not n1. The `for k in range(1, n1 + 1)` will stop early when return. So list length small. This is simpler than while. But if n1=1e6 and no cycle impossible if n1 > len(s2); if n1 <= len(s2), list length <=100. So `range(1, n1+1)` is fine. It doesn't preallocate. Let's use for loop for readability. Need ensure if no cycle and n1=100, list length 101. Good.

Let's test for loop with n1=0: range(1,1) empty, return count[0]//n2=0. Good.

```
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        allowed = set(s2)
        if not allowed.issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in allowed)

        repeat_count = [0]
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = 0
        count = 0

        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count.append(count)

            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle_len = k - start
                cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
                remaining = n1 - start

                total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]

                return total // n2

            seen[j] = k

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2
```

Let's consider if there is any hidden test where `n1` is extremely large and `range(1, n1+1)` would be huge if no cycle? But no cycle impossible if n1 > len(s2). Let's prove: At each copy, j is one of len(s2) values. We start with seen {0}. If no cycle through k copies, then j values at copies 1..k are all not in seen at the time, so they are all distinct and none is 0. There are len(s2)-1 nonzero values. Thus no cycle can last at most len(s2)-1 copies. So if n1 > len(s2)-1, cycle found. Therefore range length at most len(s2) if no return? Actually if n1 = len(s2)-1, no cycle possible and loop runs len(s2)-1. If n1 = len(s2), by pigeonhole, cycle found at k=len(s2) because there are len(s2) possible j values and we have seen 0 plus k distinct nonzero values; if k=len(s2), that's 1+len(s2) distinct? Let's formalize: possible j values count L. Initial seen contains one value (0). Each non-repeating k adds a new value not in seen. After k copies without repeat, seen size = 1+k. Since max size L, no repeat possible only if 1+k <= L, i.e., k <= L-1. So loop without return at most L-1. Thus if n1 >= L, return before or at k=L. So range up to n1 but returns early. Good.

Now, let's think about if the problem's return should be `total // n2` or `total // n2` but if `total` is count of s2 blocks in S1, and S2 = s2 repeated n2 times, then max m = floor(total / n2). Yes.

Let's maybe recall LeetCode 466 official solution:
```
int getMaxRepetitions(string s1, int n1, string s2, int n2) {
    vector<int> repeatCnt(n1 + 1, 0);
    vector<int> nextIdx(n1 + 1, 0);
    int j = 0, cnt = 0;
    for (int k = 1; k <= n1; ++k) {
        for (char c : s1) {
            if (c == s2[j]) {
                ++j;
                if (j == s2.size()) {
                    j = 0;
                    ++cnt;
                }
            }
        }
        repeatCnt[k] = cnt;
        nextIdx[k] = j;
        for (int start = 0; start < k; ++start) {
            if (nextIdx[start] == j) {
                int prevCnt = repeatCnt[start];
                int patternCnt = (repeatCnt[k] - prevCnt) * ((n1 - start) / (k - start));
                int remainCnt = repeatCnt[start + (n1 - start) % (k - start)] - prevCnt;
                return (prevCnt + patternCnt + remainCnt) / n2;
            }
        }
    }
    return repeatCnt[n1] / n2;
}
```
Our dict version is equivalent. Good.

No, length = k+1. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with the greedy matching of s2 blocks if s2 has overlapping with itself? Greedy resetting j to 0 after complete block is standard for counting non-overlapping? Wait, subsequence matching of repeated s2 blocks: When one s2 block is completed, the next s2 block starts immediately after the last matched character. This is non-overlapping in terms of matched positions, but the pattern s2 may overlap with itself; however, because we are counting consecutive blocks of s2, each block must be a separate subsequence, and they cannot share characters. Greedy earliest completion maximizes the number of blocks. Is there any scenario where not completing a block as soon as possible (i.e., using a later character for the last char of s2) could allow more total blocks? For subsequence matching, to maximize number of copies of a fixed string, you should match each copy as early as possible. This is a standard exchange argument. So yes.

Let's test an overlapping pattern: s2="aba", S1="ababa". Greedy count "aba" blocks:

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the code if `s1` is "abc" and `s
</think>

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        allowed = set(s2)
        if not allowed.issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in allowed)

        repeat_count = [0]
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = 0
        count = 0

        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count.append(count)

            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle_len = k - start
                cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
                remaining = n1 - start

                total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]

                return total // n2

            seen[j] = k

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2
```

class Solution:
    def getMaxRepetitions(self, s1: str, n1: int, s2: str, n2: int) -> int:
        allowed = set(s2)
        if not allowed.issubset(set(s1)):
            return 0

        s1 = ''.join(c for c in s1 if c in allowed)

        repeat_count = [0]
        seen = {0: 0}
        j = 0
        count = 0

        for k in range(1, n1 + 1):
            for ch in s1:
                if ch == s2[j]:
                    j += 1
                    if j == len(s2):
                        j = 0
                        count += 1

            repeat_count.append(count)

            if j in seen:
                start = seen[j]
                cycle_len = k - start
                cycle_count = count - repeat_count[start]
                remaining = n1 - start

                total = repeat_count[start] + cycle_count * (remaining // cycle_len)
                total += repeat_count[start + remaining % cycle_len] - repeat_count[start]

                return total // n2

            seen[j] = k

        return repeat_count[n1] // n2
